117. **Counting modulo p via group actions (Wedderburn/Witt, Wielandt's Sylow proof)** [`WEDD`, 2 items] — *before:* - — *after:* Wedderburn 1905 (Witt 1931 proof): every finite division ring is commutative; Sylow existence for every group (Wielandt 1959 proof) — *last step:* Orbit counting: class equation with cyclotomic divisibility (Witt); Wielandt: C(p^a m, p^a) is not divisible by p, so some orbit on p^a-subsets has size prime to p and its stabiliser is a Sylow subgroup. — *refs:* Wielandt, Arch. Math. 10 (1959) 401-402; Witt, Abh. Math. Sem. Hamburg 8 (1931) 413
118. **Interlacing families: Ramanujan graphs of every degree; Kadison-Singer** [`MSS`, 3 items] — *before:* Lubotzky-Phillips-Sarnak / Margulis 1988 (arithmetic constructions for d-1 prime), Morgenstern (prime powers); Kadison-Singer reduced to Weaver's conjecture — *after:* Marcus-Spielman-Srivastava 2013/2015: bipartite Ramanujan graphs of every degree d >= 3 (and every number of vertices, 2015); Kadison-Singer resolved — *last step:* Method of interlacing families: the expected characteristic polynomial of a random signing/lift is real-rooted and its largest root bounds that of some realisation (mixed characteristic polynomials, real stability). — *refs:* arXiv:1304.4132 (Marcus-Spielman-Srivastava, Interlacing families I: all degrees); arXiv:1306.3969 (MSS, Interlacing families II: Kadison-Singer); MSS, Interlacing families IV: bipartite Ramanujan graphs of all sizes (FOCS 2015 / SIAM J. Comput.)
119. **Hardy-Littlewood: every large non-square n = p + m^2 (stuck mirror with a square obstruction)** [`HLSQ`, 2 items] — *before:* Almost all n (Davenport-Heilbronn-type exceptional-set results); best located power saving E(X) << X^0.982 (H. Li, Acta Math. Hungar. 99 (2003)) — *after:* STILL OPEN for every large non-square n; squares are excluded because n = a^2 has at most one representation (n - m^2 = (a-m)(a+m) is prime only for m = a-1, needing 2a-1 prime), so the asymptotic count fails there — *last step:* Not achieved: main term ~ sqrt(n)/log n is a power, yet it is a binary problem (one prime variable, one square) with no pointwise minor-arc control. — *refs:* Hardy-Littlewood, Partitio Numerorum III, Acta Math. 44 (1923) (Conjecture H); H. Li, The exceptional set for the sum of a prime and a square, Acta Math. Hungar. 99 (2003) 123-142
120. **Siegel/Brjuno/Yoccoz: from almost-every to an exact characterisation** [`YOCCOZ`, 2 items] — *before:* Siegel 1942: linearisation for Diophantine rotation numbers (full measure) — *after:* Brjuno 1971: every Brjuno number suffices; Yoccoz 1988: for the quadratic family the Brjuno condition is also necessary (exact characterisation of the exceptional set) — *last step:* KAM/Newton iteration with small-divisor control (Brjuno sum of log q_{n+1}/q_n); renormalisation and a sharp construction of non-linearisable examples for non-Brjuno numbers. — *refs:* Yoccoz, Asterisque 231 (1995) 1-88 (result from 1988)
121. **Lehmer's conjecture tau(n) != 0 (stuck mirror; congruences give non-vanishing on classes)** [`LEHMER`, 2 items] — *before:* Lehmer 1947: tau(n) != 0 for n below a bound, reduction to primes; Serre: density of p with tau(p) = 0 is 0 — *after:* STILL OPEN for every p; Ramanujan-type congruences (tau(n) = sigma_11(n) mod 691, and others mod 2^k, 3^k, 5^k, 7, 23) prove tau(p) != 0 on all p outside a thin set of residue classes, and computations extend verification (verifier suggested a different mod-691 form; rejected: the classical congruence is tau(n) = sigma_11(n) mod 691) — *last step:* Congruences for Fourier coefficients modulo small primes (Swinnerton-Dyer's mod-l Galois images) exclude vanishing on most classes; the remaining classes need a different input. — *refs:* Lehmer, Duke Math. J. 14 (1947) 429-433; Serre, Publ. IHES 54 (1981) (Chebotarev applications)
122. **Elkies: every elliptic curve over Q has infinitely many supersingular primes** [`ELKIES`, 3 items] — *before:* Serre: density zero of supersingular primes for non-CM curves; infinitude unknown — *after:* Elkies 1987: infinitely many supersingular primes for every elliptic curve over Q — *last step:* Supersingular primes divide values of Hilbert class polynomials H_D(j(E)) and must be inert in Q(sqrt -D); a sign/size argument on H_D(j(E)) plus Jacobi-symbol parity forces a prime factor in the inert class outside any given finite set. — *refs:* Elkies, Invent. Math. 89 (1987) 561-568
123. **Integral Hasse principle for Markoff-type surfaces (FAILED upgrade: almost all, not every)** [`GSMARKOFF`, 2 items] — *before:* Ghosh-Sarnak: integral points on x^2+y^2+z^2 - xyz = k: the integral Hasse principle holds for almost all admissible k — *after:* Ghosh-Sarnak 2017/2022 and Loughran-Mitankin: infinitely many integral Hasse failures, some not explained by Brauer-Manin (cited in FRONTIER 2.4 as the main precedent for caution) — *last step:* Reduction theory for the Vieta-group action: integral points have orbit representatives in a fundamental set; congruence conditions can exclude all representatives for infinitely many k. — *refs:* arXiv:1706.06712 (Ghosh-Sarnak, Integral points on Markoff type cubic surfaces, Invent. Math. 229 (2022)); Loughran-Mitankin, Integral Hasse principle and strong approximation for Markoff surfaces (IMRN 2021; check)

## Items

1. **Helfgott meet-in-the-middle: an explicit 'all p > X' theorem for any ES sub-route with X ≤ 10¹⁸** — Ternary Goldbach became 'every n' only when the analytic threshold dropped below the numerical verification horizon (8.875·10³⁰). ES is verified to 10¹⁸; any effective all-large-p statement for (★), b=1, ES₅ or Type II with p₀ ≤ 10¹⁸ closes ES. — *why:* Turns the goal from 'prove for all p' into 'make some asymptotic statement effective with a concrete constant', which is how Vinogradov→Helfgott actually moved. — *first step:* Make a table: every asymptotic/conditional ES statement in FRONTIER §2.4/§5 (H⁻_BM, Thm 2.2, ES₅ Thm 3.5) with the explicit p₀ it would need and the explicit constants it currently lacks. — [high/medium; effective-threshold, verification, meta-strategy, from:TG]
2. **Explicit Burgess constants (Treviño-type) for the Legendre-sum T(p) in M_BM** — Helfgott needed fully explicit major arcs; the ES analogue is an explicit version of Thm 2.2 (M_BM(p) ≥ (3/16π² − o(1)) log²p), whose o(1) comes from Burgess on Σ(−m/p)w(m). — *why:* An explicit M_BM ≥ c·log²p for all p ≥ p₁ is a prerequisite of every H⁻_BM-type route and is purely a bookkeeping task with existing explicit Burgess inequalities. — *first step:* Treviño, 'The Burgess inequality and the least kth power non-residue', IJNT 11 (2015) 1653–1678, arXiv:1412.3062 gives explicit Burgess estimates (verified, data/z52b/upgrades_leads_check.md); check that its ranges cover the weighted hyperbolic sum T(p) and compute the resulting explicit p₁. — [high/medium; explicit-constants, burgess, BM-main-term, from:TG, seen:BRAINSTORM/z52_deep_thin_pointwise_astra.md]
3. **Smoothing as a decisive ingredient: smooth weights on (u,B,C) for a weighted R_w(p)** — Helfgott's minor arcs only closed after choosing smoothing functions optimally. ES: replace R(p) by R_w(p) = Σ w(u,B,C)1[4uBC−u−B=p] with smooth nonnegative w; R_w(p) > 0 ⇒ ES(p). — *why:* Error terms in Poisson/Voronoi-based treatments of R(p) depend heavily on sharp cut-offs of the hyperbolic region; smoothing is free for a positivity statement. — *first step:* Numerically compare Var_p(R_w(p)/M_w(p)) over primes near 10⁷ for sharp vs. Gaussian/Beurling–Selberg weights; pick the weight minimising the left tail. — [high/cheap; smoothing, numerics, weights, from:TG]
4. **Vinogradov bilinear decomposition of the (★) count** — Vinogradov's Type I/II bilinear sums made ternary Goldbach unconditional. R(p) = Σ_C #{d | 4Cp+1 : d ≡ −1 (4C)} is a sum over factorizations ab = 4Cp+1; split by the sizes of a, b, C into 'Type I' (one factor short: congruence sums in C) and 'Type II' (both medium: bilinear forms in (a,b)). — *why:* Gives a structured place where cancellation must be found; the Type I part may be treatable pointwise in p by elementary counting. — *first step:* Write R(p) = Σ_{a,b} α_a β_b 1[ab ≡ 1 mod 4C, ab = 4Cp+1] with dyadic ranges; compute numerically which range carries the solutions for p ~ 10⁸–10¹⁰. — [med/medium; bilinear-forms, type-I-II, decomposition, from:TG]
5. **Sparsity index: why binary Goldbach is stuck and ES is worse** — Binary Goldbach has a main term n/log²n among ~n terms and never got pointwise; ES has main term ≍ log²p among ≍ p log p 'trial events' (PARITY_AUDIT trial form). — *why:* Quantifies how far ES is from any problem ever upgraded; directs search to reformulations with denser events (fewer trials, larger main term). — *first step:* Define sparsity = log(main term)/log(#trials) for each ES reformulation (★, b=1, ES₅, e-slice, I3 words) and tabulate; look for the least sparse one. — [high/cheap; meta-strategy, binary-problem, reformulation, from:BG]
6. **Montgomery–Vaughan power-saving exceptional set for ES or (★)** — MV proved E_Goldbach(N) ≪ N^{1−δ} by large sieve + zero density + Siegel-zero analysis; ES has only E(N) ≪ N exp(−c(log N)^{3/4}) and 3/4 is sharp for CRT sieves (EXCEPTIONAL_KARY3). — *why:* A power-saving exceptional set would need exactly the non-CRT input the campaign identified (residue-dense large sieve); MV is a model of how that input looked for Goldbach. — *first step:* Read MV §§3–6 and identify the step that becomes 'large sieve over moduli 4uB with p in the variable'; check whether the ES analogue of their Siegel-zero case analysis is available. — [med/big; exceptional-set, large-sieve, power-saving, from:BG]
7. **Goldbach exceptions are tied to one exceptional character — are ES/(★) failures tied to a character?** — In MV the only way to have many Goldbach exceptions is a Siegel zero. Analogue: (★)-failures (14 primes ≤ 10¹⁰) and b=1 failures (193, 2521, 66529) might share an extreme value of a single character statistic (e.g. least non-residue ℓ(p), or L(1, (·/p))). — *why:* If failures of sub-routes are 'explained' by one character, a Deuring–Heilbronn-type dichotomy becomes plausible. — *first step:* For the 17 known sub-route failures and random controls compute ℓ(p), Σ_{m≤100}(m/p)/m, L(1,(·/p)) and the class number of Q(√p); compare ranks. — [high/cheap; exceptional-character, numerics, least-nonresidue, from:BG, seen:BRAINSTORM/README.md]
8. **Chen switching for ES: Type I ↔ Type II role switch to upper-bound 'near misses'** — Chen bounded the bad P₃ contribution by sieving the switched set. ES: a 'near miss' at level C is a divisor of 4Cp+1 in a wrong class; switching d ↔ (4Cp+1)/d and C ↔ another parametrisation (Type I ↔ Type II) may convert hard lower bounds into upper bounds of a switched count. — *why:* Lower bound = (computable total) − (upper-bounded bad part) is the only robust way sieves get existence. — *first step:* List all involutions/bijections between ES certificate sets (Type I/II, (★)↔b=1, level switching) and check whether any maps near-misses of one family to near-misses with a computable total in another. — [med/medium; switching, sieve, type-I-II, from:CHEN]
9. **Almost-prime levels: choose C with 4Cp+1 = q₁q₂ (two primes) and study the class of q₁ mod 4C** — Chen-type results produce almost-primes in linear sequences. If 4Cp+1 = q₁q₂, the certificate condition at level C is q₁ ≡ −1 (mod 4C), a single-prime class condition. — *why:* Reduces a divisor-class problem to an equidistribution question for prime factors of almost-primes in a progression, a better-studied object. — *first step:* For p ~ 10⁸ and C ≤ 10⁵ measure: fraction of C with 4Cp+1 = P₂, and the rate at which a factor hits −1 mod 4C versus the model 2/φ(4C). — [med/cheap; almost-primes, numerics, chen-sieve, from:CHEN]
10. **Schnirelmann/Mann in the space of fractions: ES as U + (U+U) ∋ 4/n** — Schnirelmann turned a positive density for P+P into 'every n'. ES asks 4/n ∈ U+U+U (U = unit fractions). Translate to: 4/n − U meets U+U, and look for Mann-type sumset theorems for sets of rationals with structured denominators. — *why:* 4-term ES is trivial (4/p = 1/(k+1) + 3/(p(k+1)) and 3/m always splits into ≤ 3 terms), so the problem is exactly the 'order 3 vs order 4' gap of a basis — a classical additive-combinatorics shape. — *first step:* Formalize U+U as {a/b : b has a divisor pair (d,e) with d+e ≡ 0 mod a}; study the measure/density of 4/n − U ∩ (U+U) for n in a dyadic range. — [low/medium; additive-basis, sumset, egyptian-fractions, from:SCHN]
11. **Diophantine approximation spectrum of 4/p by three unit fractions** — Schnirelmann-type arguments start from approximate representations. Define δ(p) = min_{x,y,z} |4/p − 1/x − 1/y − 1/z| over x,y,z ≤ p^A; ES(p) ⟺ δ(p) = 0. Study the gap structure of the set of values near 4/p. — *why:* If near-solutions are abundant at scale p^{−k} and the set of 3-term sums has a 'no isolated gap' property near 4/p, existence might follow by a continuity/pigeonhole step. — *first step:* Compute the k smallest nonzero |4/p − Σ| for p ≤ 10⁵ and look for universal patterns in the residual numerators. — [low/cheap; approximation, numerics, egyptian-fractions, from:SCHN]
12. **Powers-of-two slice: ES solutions with x = 2^k** — Goldbach–Linnik used the lacunary set of powers of 2. ES slice: 4/p = 1/2^k + 1/y + 1/z needs (2^{k+2} − p)/(p·2^k) to split into two unit fractions; e.g. via the divisor p it needs (2^{k+2} − p) | (2^i + 1) for some i ≤ k+2. — *why:* A lacunary, explicit family of candidate first denominators with rich multiplicative structure (2^i+1 factors via cyclotomy) — the ES analogue of Linnik's 2^v. — *first step:* Compute, for p ≡ 1 (mod 24) up to 10⁷, the fraction of p having an ES solution with a power-of-2 denominator and the distribution of the least k. — [high/cheap; lacunary, numerics, explicit-family, from:GL]
13. **Measure of joint failure along a lacunary level sequence C ∈ {2^j}** — Linnik's key estimate is the small measure of α where Σ e(2^v α) is large. Analogue: the set of p failing (★) at every level C = 2^j, j ≤ J, should have density ≈ Π_j(1 − P_j); measure its correlation structure. — *why:* Lacunary levels give nearly independent constraints (moduli 2^{j+2} are nested though!) — quantifies how much independence nested moduli really give. — *first step:* Compute failure indicators at C = 2^j for p ≤ 10⁸ and estimate joint failure probabilities versus the independent model. — [med/cheap; lacunary, independence, numerics, from:GL]
14. **Universal-form reduction: a map from representations by a universal quadratic form to ES certificates** — Linnik/Watson reduced 7 cubes to ternary forms; Hilbert used identities + Lagrange. Seek a quadratic form Q_p (or a fixed universal Q) and an explicit rational map Φ such that every representation Q(v) = g(p) yields an ES solution for p; universality/three-squares then proves ES. — *why:* Not a polynomial identity in p (so Mordell–Schinzel does not apply directly); the map may depend on the representation. Must be compatible with T1 (Type I/II fail at odd squares). — *first step:* Search small Φ: take ES (★) triples (u,B,C) for many p and test whether u, B, C, or 4uC−1 are systematically sums of two/three squares with coordinates linked to p. — [med/big; quadratic-forms, reduction, identities, from:WAR, seen:BRAINSTORM/z52_lit_thin_pointwise.md]
15. **Smooth-number restriction (Vaughan–Wooley) with smooth moduli in APs (Drappeau/Harper)** — Smooth numbers drove progress on G(k). ES: restrict certificates to smooth (u,B) or smooth 4C, where distribution in progressions to large moduli is better (friable Bombieri–Vinogradov beyond 1/2). — *why:* Smooth moduli are where the strongest level-of-distribution results live; ES certificates are numerically often smooth. — *first step:* Drappeau, 'Théorèmes de type Fouvry–Iwaniec pour les entiers friables', Compositio 151 (2015), arXiv:1307.7554 (verified) gives beyond-√ distribution for friable integers; check whether it applies to sums Σ_{C smooth} #{d | 4Cp+1, d ≡ −1 (4C)}; measure numerically the fraction of (★) solutions with y-smooth C for y = p^{0.1}. — [med/medium; smooth-numbers, level-of-distribution, from:WAR, seen:data/z52b/upgrades_leads_check.md, seen:BRAINSTORM/README.md]
16. **g(4)=19 descent: analytic threshold + greedy reduction to the verified range** — BDD reduced every n below 10³⁶⁷ to the verified range by subtracting biquadrates. ES has no subtraction structure, but the e-slice form reduces ES(p) to a property of N = (p+e)/4 < p; formalize a 'descent' that moves a hard instance to a smaller one. — *why:* If certificate existence at p could be read off from data at N ≈ p/4 that is itself verifiable recursively, verification scales. — *first step:* Measure: for the hardest primes (DISPROOF_AVENUES list) how many e ≤ 1000 give N = (p+e)/4 with ≥ k divisors; is there always an e with N highly composite? — [med/cheap; descent, verification, e-slice, from:WAR]
17. **Ternary-form reduction with Duke–Schulze-Pillot as the closing theorem** — Linnik's 7 cubes: identity reduces the problem to n − (cubes) being represented by a ternary quadratic form in an AP, closed by ternary-form theory. ES: find an ES-certificate family equivalent to 'some N(p) is primitively represented by a positive ternary form in a fixed genus'. — *why:* DSP gives every sufficiently large n in the spinor genus (ineffectively) — an 'all large p' ES theorem even if ineffective would be new. — *first step:* Search the (★)/b=1/ES₅ certificate shapes for a sum-of-three-squares interpretation (e.g. 4Cp+1 = d d' with d = x²+y²+z²-type constraint); test on data. — [med/big; ternary-forms, reduction, ineffective, from:LIN7]
18. **Divisor counts in a class as coset theta series of the split form xy** — Watson's trick works with sums of squares in arithmetic progressions (coset theta series). D_t^−(N) = #{xy = N, x ≡ −1 (t)} is a representation number of the indefinite binary form xy on a lattice coset. — *why:* Puts ES level counts into the language of (indefinite) lattice cosets where theta-lifts and Siegel–Weil apply; METAPLECTIC_ES already gives the generating function. — *first step:* Write Σ_N D_t^−(N)q^N as a coset theta series of xy and compare with METAPLECTIC_ES Thm K; then try a theta lift to a ternary/quaternary positive form. — [high/medium; theta-series, lattice-cosets, split-form, from:LIN7, seen:BRAINSTORM/z39_kronecker_levels.md]
19. **Decoupling/energy for the hyperbolic ES surface {4uBC − u − B}** — BDG decoupling bounds moments of exponential sums over curves. Additive energy of the ES-word set (#{w₁ = w₂} with w = 4uBC−u−B ≤ N) controls the second moment Σ_n R(n)². — *why:* Second-moment control is the ingredient of every almost-all → pointwise attempt via variance; decoupling-type bounds for this specific surface could be sharp. — *first step:* Compute the energy E(N) = Σ_{n≤N} R(n)² for N up to 10⁷ and fit N·(log N)^a; compare with what a decoupling inequality for (u, B, uB) would predict. — [med/medium; decoupling, energy, second-moment, from:VMVT, seen:data/energy_note]
20. **Efficient congruencing in the level variable** — Wooley's method lifts solutions p-adically and compares congruence classes at several scales. 21. **Linnik dispersion for the fixed-p level sum Σ_C D(4Cp+1; −1 mod 4C)** — Linnik proved an asymptotic for every n of #{n = p + x² + y²} = Σ_p r₂(n − p): a pointwise result for a sum of divisor-type functions. R(p) is a sum over C of restricted divisor counts of 4Cp+1. — *why:* The dispersion method is one of the few techniques giving pointwise-in-n asymptotics for sums of arithmetic functions over a sparse family — exactly ES's shape, though the main term is only polylog. — *first step:* Compute Disp(p) = Σ_C (D_C(p) − 2/φ(4C)·τ*(4Cp+1))² for test p and compare with M(p)²; if Disp ≪ M², a dispersion-type proof is not excluded numerically. — [med/medium; dispersion, pointwise, divisor-sums, from:HLP, seen:BRAINSTORM/tech_smooth_level.md]
22. **Divisor function in APs to a single modulus 4p as the base asymptotic** — Σ_{C≤X} τ(4Cp+1) is a divisor sum in the progression 1 mod 4p; asymptotics are known for X ≥ (4p)^{2/3+ε} (Selberg/Hooley/Heath-Brown/Fouvry–Iwaniec-type). The ES count refines τ to the class d ≡ −1 (mod 4C). — *why:* Starts from a pointwise-in-p theorem that is already true, and asks for a twist by the 'self-referential' modulus 4C — a precise, new-looking question. — *first step:* Write D(4Cp+1; −1 mod 4C) via additive characters mod 4C and apply Voronoi summation in C to the twisted divisor sum; identify which terms are new. — [high/medium; divisor-in-AP, voronoi, pointwise, from:HLP, seen:BRAINSTORM/README.md]
23. **Lagrange descent on near-certificates via a composition law** — Lagrange got every p by producing m·p = sum of 4 squares with m < p and descending m using Euler's identity. ES needs a composition law on 'defective' certificates (defect m) that lowers |m|. — *why:* Descent is the oldest almost→every mechanism; ES lacks an obvious composition, but the SL₂ form (matrices [[a,p],[c,d]]) has matrix multiplication. — *first step:* Define defect for SL₂-form near-certificates (e.g. congruence a ≡ −1 only mod c/m); test whether products/quotients of two near-certificates with the same defect yield smaller defect. — [med/medium; descent, composition-law, SL2, from:LAG]
24. **Chord–tangent operations on the ES cubic surface family** — Euler's identity is a composition law on norm forms. 25. **Pigeonhole over complementary divisor sets: |Div(N₁)| + |Div(N₂)| > φ(e) forces a certificate** — Lagrange's lemma: the sets {x²} and {−1−y²} each have (p+1)/2 elements so they meet. ES e-slice: for a coprime split N = N₁N₂ (N = (p+e)/4), if |Div(N₁) mod e| + |Div(N₂) mod e| > φ(e), some a | N₁ and b | N₂ satisfy a ≡ −b, so −1 ∈ Rat_e(N). — *why:* A clean sufficient condition reducing ES to finding e with N highly composite relative to φ(e). — *first step:* For p in the hard list, search e ≤ 10⁴ maximising (|Div(N₁)|+|Div(N₂)|)/φ(e) over coprime splits; report how close to 1 the best ratio gets. — [high/cheap; pigeonhole, e-slice, divisor-classes, from:LAG, seen:BRAINSTORM/s6_divisor_pigeonhole.md]
26. **Zagier-type involution with a unique fixed point on an ES-related finite set** — Zagier: a finite set with two involutions; one has exactly one fixed point ⇒ odd cardinality ⇒ the other has a fixed point = representation. 27. **Naive parity of a level count is trivial — record the lemma and the needed asymmetry** — D_t^−(tp+1) is even unless tp+1 = s² with s ≡ −1 (mod t), i.e. unless p = t − 2; so parity of single-level counts carries no information (pairing d ↔ N/d). Zagier needed an asymmetric second involution. — *why:* Clears a trap: any parity proof needs a set where the obvious involution is broken (e.g. by a non-symmetric constraint like d < N/d with an extra condition). — *first step:* Write the lemma; then search for natural subsets (e.g. d in a half-interval, or d with an extra character condition) where the count is not forced even and test its parity distribution. — [high/cheap; parity, lemma, negative-result, from:TWOSQ]
28. **Dedekind's Gaussian-integer proof analogue: an order where ES(p) ⟺ p is a norm** — Fermat two squares follows from ℤ[i] being Euclidean. 30. **Small vector + congruence ⇒ equality, for the cubic ES polynomial (Ankeny-style)** — Thue/Ankeny: if F(v) ≡ 0 mod M and |F(v)| < M then F(v) = 0. 31. **Class number one in the genus: search for an ES-encoding lattice with a one-class genus** — Gauss's three squares: local solvability + one class in the genus of x²+y²+z² ⇒ global. 32. **Auxiliary-prime skeleton of the three-squares proof applied to ES windows** — Gauss/Dirichlet: choose an auxiliary prime q with (−n/q) = 1, build a form of determinant 1 representing n, then use one class. 223. **Extreme-value statistics of the least certificate parameter** — Linnik-type theorems bound the least prime in a class. Measure the least certificate parameter (least C for (★), least e for windows) as p grows and fit an extreme-value law; a provable bound of the observed shape would be the 'Linnik constant' of ES. — *why:* Calibrates which quantitative statements are plausible (e.g. C(p) ≤ (log p)^A for all p?) before trying to prove any. — *first step:* From the repo's census (and adversarial primes at 10¹⁸–10²⁵) tabulate max over p ≤ X of least C and least e versus log X. — [high/cheap; least-certificate, extreme-values, numerics, from:LINNIK, seen:DISPROOF_AVENUES.md]
224. **Integral Brauer–Manin for the (u,B,C) threefold as a family of affine conics over the C-line** — For fixed C, (★) is the hyperbola (4uC − 1)(4BC − 1) = 4Cp + 1, an affine conic. Integral Hasse/BM results for families of affine quadrics (Colliot-Thélène–Xu, strong approximation with BM) treat such fibrations; analyse the total space over the C-line. — *why:* BL computed Br for the ES surface; the (u,B,C) threefold is a different model where fibrewise integral BM is classical. — *first step:* Compute the Brauer group of the generic fibre (a split conic? then trivial) and the integral obstruction for the total space; compare with BL's ℤ/2. — [med/medium; integral-points, brauer-manin, conic-bundles, from:HASSE]
225. **Vinogradov's smooth-number trick for divisor classes** — Vinogradov bounded n(p): if every ℓ ≤ y is a QR, all y-smooth numbers are QRs, contradicting character-sum bounds since smooth numbers are plentiful. ES: if level C fails, no divisor of 4Cp+1 is ≡ −1 (mod 4C); if additionally we know the classes of small primes, smooth parts of 4Cp+1 are constrained. Count C for which 4Cp+1 has a y-smooth divisor in a forced class. — *why:* Transfers a proven every-p argument shape (n(p) bounds) to divisor classes, using smooth numbers in APs (Granville, Harper). — *first step:* Model: fix the classes mod 4C of primes ≤ y; count C ≤ X such that 4Cp+1 has a y-smooth divisor d ≡ −1 (mod 4C) using smooth-number distribution; compare with data. — [med/medium; smooth-numbers, vinogradov-trick, least-nonresidue, from:BURGESS]
226. **Kummer-style 'regular' primes for ES: an invariant κ(p) that makes ES provable** — Kummer proved FLT for regular primes (p ∤ class number) long before Wiles. Look for an arithmetic invariant κ(p) (class number of ℚ(√−p), 2-rank/4-rank, parity of the fundamental unit norm of ℚ(√p), ℓ(p)) such that ES (or a sub-route) is provable for all p with κ(p) in some set, giving an infinite every-p family. — *why:* Partial every-p theorems on natural infinite sets of Mordell-hard primes do not yet exist; Kummer's theorem is the historical model. — *first step:* Correlate (★)/ES₅ counts with κ(p) candidates for p ≤ 10⁸ and look for an invariant whose small/large values guarantee many certificates. — [med/cheap; regular-primes, invariants, intermediate-target, from:FLT]
227. **Limiting distribution of R(p)/M_BM(p) (a Sato–Tate/Cohen–Lenstra-type law)** — Sato–Tate identified the exact limiting law of normalised a_p. For ES, determine the limiting distribution of R(p)/M_BM(p) (and of f(p)/log³p) as p → ∞ through Mordell-hard primes; its behaviour near 0 is what decides ES heuristically. — *why:* A precise conjectural law with a computable left tail sharpens the heuristic case for ES and indicates which normalisation is 'right'. — *first step:* Histogram R(p)/M_BM(p) for primes in windows at 10⁶, 10⁸, 10¹⁰; test convergence and fit the left tail. — [high/cheap; limiting-distribution, left-tail, numerics, from:SATO]
228. **Ramanujan-type congruences for ES counts on progressions** — Ramanujan/Ono congruences say p(An + B) ≡ 0 (mod m). Search for progressions An + B on which f_II(n) or R(n) is always ≡ 0 (mod m) (m = 2, 3, 4, 8) beyond the trivial evenness; such congruences would reveal modular structure that might also produce nonzero congruences at primes. — *why:* Systematic congruence mining is cheap and has historically uncovered hidden modular structure. — *first step:* Compute f_II(n), R(n) for n ≤ 10⁷ and test all A ≤ 840, B mod A for congruences mod 2, 3, 4, 8. — [high/cheap; congruences, data-mining, modular-structure, from:ONO]
229. **ES as an orbifold Euler-characteristic realisation problem (triangle groups, Hurwitz spaces)** — Chen's Markoff theorem is a statement about Hurwitz spaces. Note: 1/x + 1/y + 1/z − 1 is the orbifold Euler characteristic of the sphere with cone points of orders x, y, z. So ES(p) ⟺ some triangle orbifold S²(x,y,z) has χ^orb = (4 − p)/p, i.e. hyperbolic area 2π(p − 4)/p. — *why:* Gives ES a geometric/topological reading where covering-space and Hurwitz-space divisibility arguments (as in Chen) might apply. — *first step:* Work out what Riemann–Hurwitz says for covers of S²(x,y,z) with χ = (4 − p)/p (index must be divisible by p); look for a moduli space whose nonemptiness is equivalent to ES(p). — [med/medium; triangle-groups, orbifolds, hurwitz-spaces, from:MARKOFF]
230. **Prime-capture problems: every prime is caught by an infinite family of Chebotarev conditions** — Chebotarev forbids any finite family of splitting conditions from catching every prime; ES Type II (abelian conditions p ≡ −(u+B) mod 4uB) and Type I (dihedral conditions via ring class fields) are infinite families with conductors up to ≍ p. List known 'every prime is captured by a family with conductor ≤ f(p)' theorems (e.g. least non-residue: some ℓ ≤ p^{0.152} has (p/ℓ) = −1) and place ES among them. — *why:* Identifies the closest proven 'prime-capture' statements and how their conductor bounds compare with what ES needs. — *first step:* Write the conductor/size of the capturing family for ES sub-routes and for known capture theorems (Burgess, Linnik, Vinogradov) in one table. — [med/cheap; prime-capture, chebotarev, conductors, from:CFT]
231. **Class-number parity (genus theory, Rédei) as the source of oddness for a Zagier-type argument** — Heath-Brown's/Zagier's proofs use a set whose size parity is forced. Natural sets attached to p have sizes tied to class numbers h(−4p), h(−8p), whose parities/2-parts are governed by genus theory and Rédei symbols. Find an ES-related finite set whose cardinality equals (up to known even terms) such a class number with known parity. — *why:* Supplies the 'odd cardinality' input a parity proof needs from established theory. — *first step:* Compute |S_p| for candidate sets (e.g. reduced forms of disc −16p with an extra congruence) and compare parity with h(−4p), h(−8p) and Rédei-symbol predictions. — [low/medium; parity, class-numbers, genus-theory, from:TWOSQ]
232. **Polynomial certificate families in the Gaussian variables (a,b), p = a² + b²** — Mordell–Schinzel forbids polynomial identities in n covering the square classes; identities in the variables (a, b) with p = a² + b² are a different class. T1 requires that at odd-square points (Pythagorean (a,b)) such an identity yield only non-Type-I/II (imprimitive) solutions, which is not obviously impossible. — *why:* A genuinely new parametrisation space for identity searches, exactly where the classical obstruction may not bite. — *first step:* Search low-degree X, Y, Z ∈ ℚ[a,b] with 4XYZ = (a²+b²)(XY+YZ+ZX) identically and X,Y,Z positive integral on a congruence region; check behaviour at Pythagorean points. — [med/medium; identities, gaussian-integers, mordell-schinzel, from:FI]
233. **Genus-theoretic parametrisations: Mordell-hard primes as values of principal-genus forms** — Gauss's genus theory: p is a square mod 840-type moduli iff p lies in principal genera of the relevant discriminants, so p = Q(x, y) for a form Q in a principal genus (disc −4·210 etc.). Seek certificate families polynomial in (x, y) for p = Q(x, y), exploiting that Mordell-hard classes are exactly where these representations exist. — *why:* Turns 'square classes' from an obstacle into a parametrisation: hard primes come with extra representations. — *first step:* For Mordell-hard primes ≤ 10⁷, compute representations by forms of discriminants dividing −4·840 and correlate (x, y) with known certificates (u, B, C). — [med/cheap; genus-theory, parametrisation, square-classes, from:THREESQ]
234. **Large prime factors in the AP {4Cp + 1 : C ≤ K} (Sylvester–Schur/Shorey–Tijdeman)** — Sylvester–Schur: products of consecutive integers have a large prime factor; Shorey–Tijdeman extend to AP terms. For ES, a single prime q | 4Cp+1 with q ≡ −1 (mod 4C) is a certificate. Large prime factors are guaranteed in the product over C ≤ K; the class condition q ≡ −1 (mod 4C) is the extra requirement. — *why:* Gives a guaranteed supply of large prime divisors; the problem reduces to a class-distribution question for these forced large primes. — *first step:* For p ~ 10⁹, list the largest prime factor of 4Cp+1 for C ≤ 10⁴ and test the rate at which it is ≡ −1 (mod 4C) against 2/φ(4C)-type models. — [med/cheap; large-prime-factors, residue-classes, numerics, from:SYLV]
235. **Legendre-formula bookkeeping for Π_C(4Cp+1): which primes can make up the product?** — Sylvester–Schur works by bounding the contribution of small primes via Legendre's formula. For Π_{C≤K}(4Cp+1) the contribution of each prime q is explicit by CRT (q divides the terms with C ≡ c_q mod q). Compute the share of the product coming from primes q that could serve as single-prime certificates, versus the rest. — *why:* Makes the 'size argument' quantitative: if certificate-capable primes must contribute a definite share of log Π, one of them must divide some term in the right class. — *first step:* Compute Σ_{q} (number of C ≤ K with q | 4Cp+1)·log q split by whether 4c_q | q+1-type conditions can hold. — [low/cheap; size-argument, legendre-formula, CRT, from:SYLV]
236. **Erdős–Selfridge distinct-small-parts mechanism for consecutive levels** — Erdős–Selfridge: writing consecutive factors as a_i·(power) forces the a_i to be distinct and small, contradiction by counting. ES failure forces each 4Cp+1 (C = 1, …, K) into a thin multiplicative set S_C; write 4Cp+1 = a_C·b_C with b_C the S_C-compatible part and look for a forced-distinctness/smallness contradiction among the a_C. — *why:* An elementary combinatorial mechanism for consecutive terms that might bite on the explicit sets S_C at small C. — *first step:* For C ≤ 8 characterise S_C explicitly (e.g. S_1: all prime factors ≡ 1 mod 4) and test numerically how small K can be such that no prime has 4Cp+1 ∈ S_C for all C ≤ K... (there are such primes for each K; record their density decay). — [low/cheap; elementary, multiplicative-sets, consecutive-levels, from:ERDSELF]
237. **Instance transformation that preserves certificate existence but amplifies counts** — Reingold transformed every graph into an expander while preserving connectivity. ES: find a transformation p ↦ (structured object with power-many 'attempts') such that ES(p) ⟺ the object has a 'connected/nonzero' property that is robustly detectable (e.g. a spectral quantity), amplifying the polylog main term. — *why:* If the count can be amplified while preserving the zero/nonzero status, the polylog-main-term barrier (B2) changes character. — *first step:* Look at natural equivalences ES(p) ⟺ ∃e (window) and ES(p) ⟺ ∃C (level); build a graph on (e, divisors of N_e) whose connectivity property is equivalent to ES(p), and compute its spectral gap for small p. — [low/medium; amplification, derandomization, graphs, from:REINGOLD]
238. **Randomized certificate search with provable success → derandomize** — Before Reingold, connectivity was in randomized logspace. ES certificates are found by trying random e or C; a proof that a random attempt succeeds with probability ≥ 1/poly(log p) for every p would already be ES; the derandomization view asks for an explicit small 'hitting set' of attempts for every p. — *why:* Hitting-set language matches B1's analysis of bounded procedures and suggests pseudorandom-generator constructions (attempt sets that fool the certificate test). — *first step:* Define the certificate test as a function of the attempt (e, or C); compute the minimal hitting set for all primes ≤ 10⁸ and its growth. — [med/cheap; hitting-sets, derandomization, B1, from:REINGOLD, seen:POINTWISE_SIZE.md]
239. **Wielandt-style orbit counting: a group action whose non-certificate orbits have size ≡ 0 (mod m)** — Wielandt proves Sylow existence because |X| = C(p^a m, p^a) ≢ 0 (mod p), so some orbit has size prime to p. 240. **Class-equation divisibility: Φ_n(q)-type obstruction to 'no certificate'** — Witt's proof of Wedderburn: the class equation has all terms but one divisible by Φ_n(q), forcing a contradiction. 241. **Arithmetic constructions cover some classes, interlacing covers all: the LPS → MSS lesson** — LPS/Morgenstern built Ramanujan graphs arithmetically only for degrees d − 1 = prime power; MSS proved existence for every d by a non-arithmetic existence method (interlacing families). ES: Mordell identities (arithmetic) cover all non-square classes mod 840; the square classes may need a non-identity existence method of a comparable kind. — *why:* Historical precedent that 'arithmetic constructions stop at a class barrier, existence methods cross it'. — *first step:* Identify the analogue of 'expected characteristic polynomial' for ES: a polynomial built from the family of attempts (levels C or windows e) whose real-rootedness/root location certifies existence for some member. — [med/medium; interlacing, existence-method, square-classes, from:MSS, seen:BRAINSTORM/z45_interlacing_stable.md]
242. **Interlacing families over windows: real-rooted averages certify a good window** — MSS: if a family of real-rooted polynomials has a common interlacer, some member's largest root is ≤ that of the average. 243. **Exceptional set of the two-term step is tiny: 'almost all two-term + rescue' structure** — Goldbach–Linnik used that the binary Goldbach exceptional set is small enough for a sparse rescue set. 244. **Eventual periodicity of 'ES-easy' residue structures (Wilson-style)** — Wilson showed the set of orders admitting designs is eventually periodic, so finitely many residue classes need checking. 245. **Precoloured version: ES with a prescribed denominator divisible by a given small prime** — Thomassen's induction works for a stronger precoloured statement. Candidate strengthening: for every prime p and every small prime r, there is an ES solution with r | x (or r ∤ any denominator). If such a refined statement holds numerically, its extra structure might allow an induction over r or over the e-slice. — *why:* Strengthenings with an inductive parameter are how many every-n combinatorial theorems were proved. — *first step:* For p ≤ 10⁶, check which (r, condition) refinements hold for all p; record the first failure for each. — [med/cheap; strengthening, induction, refinements, from:THOM]
246. **Uncovered density of the (★) system restricted to moduli with bounded prime factors** — Hough's argument tracks how the uncovered set shrinks as primes are added to the moduli. 247. **Variance-to-mean ratio of R(p) as a quasi-independence diagnostic** — Koukoulopoulos–Maynard's key was showing overlaps are small on average (quasi-independence). For ES, the variance of R(p) over primes near X relative to its mean measures overlap; Poisson-like behaviour (ratio ≈ 1 after removing the genus fluctuation) indicates quasi-independent events. — *why:* If the residual variance after the BM/genus layer is ≈ mean, the second-moment method is near-optimal and the remaining obstacle is purely the 'single realisation' issue. — *first step:* Compute Var(R(p) − M_BM(p))/E[R(p)] in windows at 10⁶, 10⁸, 10¹⁰. — [high/cheap; variance, quasi-independence, numerics, from:DS, seen:BM_CORRECTED_COUNT.md]
248. **Primitive part of 4Cp+1 relative to smaller levels as a 'new divisor' source** — Zsigmondy isolates primes dividing a^n − 1 but no earlier term. For fixed p, call a prime factor q of 4Cp+1 'new' if q ∤ 4C′p+1 for C′ < C (equivalently c_q = C). New primes have c_q = C < q, and a new prime q is a single-prime certificate iff 4C | q + 1. Study the number of new primes at each level. — *why:* New-prime counts give an explicit, level-ordered supply of candidates with a clean certificate test. — *first step:* For p ~ 10⁸ and C ≤ 10⁵ compute the new primes at each level and the frequency of 4C | q + 1. — [med/cheap; primitive-divisors, levels, numerics, from:ZSIG]
249. **From Tijdeman (effective finiteness) to Mihăilescu (exact): bound first, then algebra** — Catalan moved from 'finitely many, bounded effectively' to 'exactly one' via algebra that did not need the bound. For ES-type finiteness targets (e.g. finitely many (★)-failures), an algebraic characterisation of failures (all 14 known (★)-failures share a property P) might give the exact list without analysis. — *why:* The 14 known (★)-failures ≤ 10¹⁰ are a finite data set where an exact algebraic description is testable. — *first step:* List the 14 primes with their factorisations of 4Cp+1 for small C, residues mod 840·k, and ℓ(p); look for a shared algebraic property and test it as a predictor above 10¹⁰. — [med/cheap; exact-classification, sub-routes, numerics, from:CAT, seen:B1_LINE.md]
250. **Euler-system-style bounds on the 'obstruction group' of certificate-free levels** — Kolyvagin bounded Selmer groups with an Euler system of Heegner points. 251. **Furstenberg-set analogue: certificate 'lines' in the (C, d) plane** — Kakeya/Furstenberg estimates bound unions of lines/tubes from below. 252. **Slice-rank of the ES 'certificate tensor' modulo q** — The cap-set proof bounds sets avoiding a pattern via the slice rank of the pattern's indicator tensor. 253. **Union-closed structure of the family of ES-solvable sets of numerators** — Gilmer's method applies to union-closed families. 254. **Is ES critical or supercritical? Fractional expectation threshold of the level hypergraph** — Park–Pham relate thresholds to expectation thresholds. The repo measures ≈ (0.44 → 0.97)·log p expected hit levels (critical regime). Compute, in a random model matching ES's level statistics, the threshold for 'some level hits' and see whether ES sits above it by a growing margin. — *why:* If ES is only 'critically' above threshold in natural models, any proof must exploit non-random structure; if supercritical, second-moment-type methods are favoured. — *first step:* Simulate the matched random model and compute P(no hit) as a function of the scaling parameter; compare with data for hard primes. — [med/cheap; thresholds, criticality, random-models, from:PARK, seen:SYNTHESIS_SPARKS9.md]
255. **Classify all 'transfer maps' between ES instances via the e-slice** — Graham's closure maps transfer representability from n to 2n+2, 2n+9. 256. **Harmonic-sum thresholds for the denominator set of ES solutions** — Liu–Sawhney: a set A ⊂ [1, N] with Σ_{n∈A} 1/n ≥ (log N)^{4/5+o(1)} contains S with Σ_{S} 1/n = 1. 257. **Antipodal structure: the swap involution u ↔ B as a free ℤ/2-action on near-certificates** — Borsuk–Ulam/Tucker need a free ℤ/2-action. 258. **Multiplicative-function structure of the level indicator at fixed p** — MR's method uses multiplicativity in n. At fixed p, the function C ↦ D_C(p) is not multiplicative in C, but N ↦ 1[N has a divisor ≡ −1 mod t] is 'multiplicative-ish' in N for fixed t. Study the Dirichlet-series/Halász structure of n ↦ D_t^−(tn + 1) for fixed t (its mean value over n is controlled by Halász-type theorems). — *why:* Halász/MR techniques give 'every interval of length h' results for multiplicative functions; level-t certificate indicators along n might be accessible. — *first step:* Express 1[D_t^−(tn+1) > 0] via the multiplicative set S_t and compute its mean in short intervals of n numerically; compare with Landau–Ramanujan-type predictions. — [med/medium; multiplicative-functions, halasz, levels, from:MR]
259. **Rédei-type reciprocity explains T1 — look for its 'higher' analogues in ES sub-routes** — Negative Pell's obstructions beyond local ones come from Rédei symbols (higher reciprocity). 333. **Switching between the two cofactors of 4Cp + 1 and the two ES types** — Chen's switching replaces a hard count by a dual count where the roles of variables swap. Find an explicit bijection/switch between near-certificates of one line and of the other, so that an upper bound for near-misses of one yields a lower bound for hits of the other. — *why:* Switching is the concrete mechanism behind complementary-route arguments. — *first step:* Search for maps (u, B, C) ↦ (d, w) between (★) data and b=1 data for the same p, and test on hard primes. — [med/cheap; switching, sub-routes, bijections, from:CHEN, seen:B1_LINE.md]
334. **Zero-free region analogue: a range of levels where no 'conspiracy' is possible** — Linnik's theorem uses a zero-free region near s = 1 for all characters of modulus ≤ Q. For ES, find a range of small levels (C ≤ C₀(p)) where the relevant characters (mod 4C) are so few that a full conspiracy against certificates would force p into explicit square-like residue classes (contradiction for non-squares). — *why:* Small levels have few characters; a 'no conspiracy at small levels' lemma would be the ES zero-free region. — *first step:* For C ≤ 30, characterise the residues of p (mod lcm(4C)) for which all these levels fail; compute the density of such residues and whether they are squares. — [high/cheap; small-levels, conspiracy, residue-classes, from:LINNIK]
335. **Box principle for windows: two windows with the same residue data combine** — Thue's lemma is a box principle. Pigeonhole windows e by the residue vector of p modulo small primes and the factorisation shape of N_e; two windows with equal data might combine (via CRT) into a certificate at the window e·e′ or at lcm, if a combination law exists. — *why:* Pigeonhole needs a combination law; windows have CRT structure that may supply one. — *first step:* Test on data: for pairs of failing windows e, e′ with compatible data, check whether e·e′ (or another derived window) succeeds more often than random. — [low/cheap; pigeonhole, windows, CRT, from:THUE]
336. **Exact formulas for divisor-class counts: Ramanujan sums expansion of D_C^−(N)** — Rademacher-type exact expansions use Kloosterman/Ramanujan sums. The class-restricted divisor count D^−_{4C}(N) has an exact finite Fourier expansion over additive characters mod 4C (Ramanujan sums c_q(·)); for the all-level sum this becomes a series Σ_q c_q-type terms, an ES 'singular series' with exact tail. — *why:* An exact Ramanujan-sum expansion of R(p) separates the main (BM) part from the rest explicitly and may be summable in closed form at small q. — *first step:* Write R(p) = Σ_q A_q(p) with A_q built from Ramanujan sums; compute A_q(p) numerically for q ≤ 100 and the remainder. — [med/cheap; exact-formula, ramanujan-sums, singular-series, from:RADEM]

338. **Commutator-style 'every element' from character ratios: target −1 in (ℤ/e)* via products of few divisor classes** — Ore-type results show every element is a product of few commutators using character bounds. 341. **Linear recurrences for minimal certificates along exponential families** — SML-type structure suggests that along n_s = a·b^s + c the minimal certificate parameters may follow linear recurrences in s on residue classes of s.
